[4:03] your garage. Pass on veneered particle board. It won't take sanding, and once that thin face layer is gone, there's nothing underneath worth saving. Pass on anything water swollen. On particle board, you'll see puffy, crumbling bottoms and feet. Now, back to that dresser in your driveway. Number four, test the old paint before you sand anything. Furniture painted before 1978 may carry lead paint, and that's not

[6:36] dust you want in your garage, on your clothes, or anywhere near your family. A lead test kit costs about $10 at the hardware store. It's a set of swabs. You rub one on the paint, and it changes color if lead is there. Test in a few spots, and use a utility knife to nick through to the lower layers because the oldest coat is often hiding under a newer one. If it comes up positive, my advice is to let that piece go. Unless you're ready to learn leads safe stripping, which is a whole job of its

[7:07] own. And even when the test comes up clean, treat any old paint with respect. Never dry sand it. Work outside. Wear a respirator rated for fine dust, like a P100, and keep a damp rag handy to wipe dust up instead of blowing it around. No shortcuts on this one. 10 minutes of testing tells you whether you've got a flip or a problem. Number five, clean it like you mean it. Almost nobody tells beginners this. Most paint failures on

[7:39] old furniture aren't paint problems, they're cleaning problems. Decades of hand oil, furniture polish, and kitchen grease sit on that surface, and new paint won't stick to any of it. Mix a degreaser, like a TSP substitute, with warm water according to the label and scrub every surface with a non-scratch scrub pad. Work top to bottom, including the inside of the drawers. Then wipe everything down with a rag and clean water to lift off the residue. If the

[8:10] piece has been kept up with spray furniture polish, follow up with a rag dampened with mineral spirits. The silicone in some polishes causes fish eyes, those little round craters where a new finish pulls away from a spot it can't grab. Give the piece until the next day to dry all the way through. Skip this and nothing you do later holds up. Do it and your paint grips like it was made for the piece. Moving on to number six. Reglue every loose joint. A

[8:41] wobble is the fastest way to lose a sale. The buyer puts a hand on the top, gives it a little push, and the conversation's over. So, go over the whole piece and wiggle everything. Every leg, every stretcher, which is the rail that ties the legs together, and every drawer corner. Chairs are the big one. If a joint moves, fresh glue alone won't fix it because new wood glue doesn't bond to old dried glue. You have to get down to wood. Tap the joint apart gently

[9:11] with a rubber mallet. Scrape the old glue off both surfaces with a chisel or a utility knife until you see clean wood. Spread a thin, even coat of wood glue on both sides. Put it back together and clamp it just snug. You want a thin bead of squeeze out, not glue running down the leg. Wipe that squeeze out off with a damp rag within a few minutes because stain won't soak into dried glue and paint shows every lump. Leave it

[9:41] clamped at least an hour and give it 24 hours before anybody sits on it or loads it up. If a joint won't come apart, a glue syringe can push fresh glue deep into the gap. It won't be as strong, but it beats a wobble. Nothing fancy about this step. It's just the one people skip. Get it right, and the piece feels solid the second a buyer touches it. Step seven on the list, make the drawers glide. The first thing most buyers do is pull a drawer. If it drags, sticks, or

[10:13] drops out of its track, the piece feels cheap, no matter how good the paint looks. Pull each drawer all the way out and look at the runners, the wooden strips the drawer rides on, and at the bottom edges of the drawer sides. Brush off the dust and grit. Then, rub a block of paraffin wax, or just the stub of an old candle, along both surfaces. It sounds too simple to work. It works. While the drawer is out, check the corners and the bottom. If a corner is

[10:43] loose, glue and clamp it the same way we did in number six. If the drawer bottom has dropped out of its groove, a couple of small wood blocks glued underneath will hold it in place. Buyers notice this. A drawer that slides open with one finger sells the whole piece. Number eight, decide paint or stain now before you fix another thing. This decision changes how you fill and how you sand. So, make it early. My rule of thumb goes

[11:13] like this. If the top has good grain and no deep damage, stain it or at least refresh the finish that's there. If it has water rings that won't come out, patches, mismatched wood, or a lot of filler coming, paint it. Plenty of flippers do both. painting the body and staining the top, and it's a sharpl looking combination. If you go with paint, chalk paint is the most forgiving choice. It grips most old finishes with very little prep, goes on thick, and

[11:44] dries to a flat, soft look. Two coats usually do it, and a quart covers a whole dresser. Milk paint is the other option, and it gives you an older hand painted look, but it comes with a catch. over an existing finish, it needs a bonding additive mixed in or it'll chip and flake. Some people want that chippy look. If you don't, use the additive. And if you're staining, a can of stain and a rag are all you need. Pick neutral colors you'd see on a furniture store

[12:14] floor. Decide now, and every step after this one has a plan. That brings us to number nine. Fill the dings and the holes. Every old piece has gouges, dents, and chipped corners. For painted pieces, wood filler handles all of it. Press it in with a putty knife. Overfill just a little because filler shrinks as it dries. And once it's hard, sand it flush with 120 grit. This is where your decision from number eight matters. Even

[12:45] filler labeled stainable almost never takes stain the way wood does. It shows up as a lighter or darker patch. So on a piece you're staining, fill only what you have to and leave the small, honest dings alone. They're part of the character. This is also the time to think about hardware, which we'll get to in number 13. If you're switching to poles with a different hole spacing, fill the old holes now from both the front and the back of the drawer before any paint goes on. Do the filling right

[13:16] and the finished surface reads as one clean piece. Number 10, sand for the finish you picked. If you're painting, you're not stripping anything. You're scuffing. Go over every surface with 120 grit, then 180 or 220, just enough to dull the shine so the paint has something to grab. If you're staining the top, you need bare wood. Start with 80 grit to cut through the old finish. Move to 120, then 180, and stop there.

[13:48] Going much finer can close up the wood so it takes stain lighter and less evenly. Always sand with the grain, meaning in the same direction the lines in the wood run. Sanding across the grain leaves scratches that stain turns into dark streaks. One warning on older tops. Some solid pieces still have a veneered top and veneer is thin. So check the edges and go light. Once you sand through it, there's no going back. When you're finished, vacuum everything,

[14:19] then wipe it down with a damp rag or a tack cloth. Dust left behind ends up in your finish. Sand smart and your finish goes on smooth the first time. Here's number 11. Put on your paint or stain the right way. For paint, use a good brush on the body and a small foam roller on the top because a roller leaves a smooth, even surface on the flat part a buyer looks at most. Thin coats beat thick ones. Put on the first coat, give it the drying time printed on

[14:50] the can, usually an hour or two in a warm garage, and then put on the second. On drawers, paint the fronts and the edges of the fronts only. Leave the sides bare or the drawer will stick. For stain, test it first on the back or the underside because the same stain looks different on every piece of wood. Wipe it on with a rag. Let it sit the few minutes the can calls for, then wipe off every bit of excess, going with the grain. Stain left sitting on top turns

[15:22] gummy. If you want it darker, let it dry and put on a second coat. Patience pays here. Two good coats look like a store piece. One thick coat looks like a garage job. Number 12, ease the worn edges. Once the paint's fully dry, take a piece of 220 grit sandpaper in your hand and make a few light passes along the edges and corners. Sharp painted edges chip the first time something bumps them, and a slightly softened edge

[15:52] looks finished instead of fragile. If you like a lightly worn look, go a touch further in the places real hands would wear it over time around the poles and along the front edge of the top. Keep it light and keep it where it makes sense. Where in the middle of a side panel looks fake. Where where hands go looks earned. A few passes and 10 minutes and the whole piece looks like it was always meant to be this way. Number 13. Swap or shine the hardware. Hardware is where

[16:24] every buyer's hand goes, and it's front and center in every photo. Ugly, broken, or mismatched poles drag a good piece down. Plan on $5 to $20 for new hardware. Before you buy, measure the center to center, meaning the distance from the middle of one screw hole to the middle of the other. Common sizes are 3 in 96 mm and 128 mm. Match that and the new poles drop right into the old holes.

[16:54] If you can't match it, you already filled those holes back in number 9. Or you can use poles with a back plate, a decorative plate that sits behind the handle and covers the old holes. Buy one extra in case one's off. And if the original hardware is solid brass and just dirty, clean it in warm soapy water with an old toothbrush instead of replacing it. Good hardware makes a $10 paint job look like a whole lot more. Number 14, seal it with a top coat. The

[17:24] top coat is what lets your work survive a buyer's first week with it. Over chalk paint, a clear furniture wax rubbed on and buffed out gives a soft sheen. On tops and anything that gets heavy use, go with a water-based wipe-on polyurethane instead because it's tougher. Over white or light paint, stay with water-based since oil-based finishes turn yellow over time. On stained wood, a wipe-on finish is about as beginner friendly as finishing gets.

[17:54] Fold a lint-free rag into a pad. Wipe on a thin coat with the grain and let it dry according to the can. Scuff lightly with 320 grit between coats and wipe the dust off. Three thin coats on the top, two everywhere else. Now, a safety point that matters. Rags soaked in oil-based stain or finish can heat up on their own as they dry and catch fire, even sitting in a trash can. Never ball them up and toss them. Lay each rag out flat outside

[18:26] on concrete until it's dry and stiff or drop them in a metal can filled with water and put the lid on. Not optional. Seal it right and the piece still looks new after the buyer gets it home. And that brings us to number 15, the one I flagged at the start. Sell it, right? Everything we've done so far is work. This is where it turns into money, and it has nothing to do with sandpaper. Your before and after photo is the listing. Set the finished piece in

[18:57] daylight near an open garage door against a plain wall. Take one shot straight on, one from an angle, and one with a drawer pulled open so the dovetails show. Those dovetales tell the buyer this isn't particle board. In the listing, say what it is honestly. Solid wood, refreshed, structurally sound with the width, depth, and height. Local pickup only. Don't call it a restored antique. Refreshed and sound is the

[19:27] honest claim, and it's the one buyers trust. Before you set a price, look at what similar refreshed pieces are listed for in your area. As a rough range, you'll have somewhere between nothing and $30 in a piece, and it goes out for $100 to $400. A dresser flip routinely brings in about $200 in profit for 3 or 4 hours of actual work. It's a quick build that solves a problem people have every time they cook. That brings us to number 12, mug racks and wine racks. I'm counting them together because they're the same build underneath, a board with pegs or holes arranged in a pattern. >> [gasps] >> They sell for $40 and material stays under 15.

[13:57] For a mug rack, drill your peg holes at a slight upward angle so the mugs settle back instead of creeping forward. For a wine rack, space the holes so bottles sit without crowding each other and test it with a real bottle before you finish anything. The same safety rule as the spice rack applies, only more so. A wall rack full of mugs or bottles goes into studs or into anchors rated for the load. No shortcuts. A contrasting peg or a band of darker

[14:28] wood along the top lifts the price. These two hang where guests stand around talking, and that's where your next customer sees your work. Now for the bonus I promised at the top, the four rules for anything that touches food. First, use hardwood only. Tight grain species like maple, cherry, and walnut hold up to knives and washing, and soft woods and scraps of mystery lumber stay out of the kitchen. Second, use a waterproof glue that's rated food safe

[15:00] once it cures because a board goes under the faucet day after day, and a glue line that lets go is a failed product. Third, finish every food surface with a food safe oil, then a coat of wax. Flood the oil on until the wood stops drinking it. Wipe off the extra and let it sit overnight before the wax goes on. Fourth, never put a film finish like varnish on a cutting surface because a knife cuts right through it and leaves flakes in the food. Follow those four on

[15:32] every piece in this video and every kitchen piece you sell will be one you'd happily put in your own kitchen. [1:30] and the store's overhead all on top of the wood itself. And because those boards are milled to sell to everybody, you only get a narrow slice of what a tree can give you. Mostly four, six and eight inch widths, lengths that usually stop at eight feet. The same three or four species every trip. Oak, maple, poplar and maybe a little walnut if you're lucky. Rough sawn lumber is the same board one step earlier. It comes off the saw with fuzzy saw marked faces

[2:01] and edges that may still wander a little. It's a touch thicker than its nominal size, so there's room to clean it up. Under that rough skin is the exact same wood. Often it's better wood because nobody has picked the stack over before you got there. [2:31] stack for what it really is, you stop paying someone else for work you can do yourself. Here's number two. Learn to count in board feet because that's the language every sawmill and hardwood dealer speaks. A board foot is a volume of wood. Picture a piece 1-ft long, 1-ft wide, and 1-in thick. That's one board foot. To figure any board, multiply the thickness in inches by the width in inches by the length in feet, then

[3:01] divide by 12. Say you have a rough board 1-in thick, 8-in wide, and 6-ft long. 1 * 8 * 6 is 48, and 48 / 12 is four board feet. Simple as that. Now the thickness part. Yards describe rough thickness in quarters of an inch. 4/4, sometimes written 4/4, means 1-in rough. 6/4 is an inch and a half. 8/4 is 2-in, and 12/4

[3:32] is 3. When you figure board feet on rough stock, you use the rough thickness, not the thickness you'll end up with. And plan on losing some to milling. A 4/4 board usually mills down to right around 3/4 of an inch finished, the same thickness as that big box board. Without this math, you walk into a dealer, see a price per board foot, and have no idea whether it's a bargain. With it, you can price a whole project on a scrap of paper before you leave the house.

[4:02] Write your cut list, add up the board feet, then tack on about 20% for waste and defects. That number is your shopping list. Write it down. [6:03] They mill trees that come down in town from storm damage, street trees, and backyard removals. They often sell wide slabs for less than you'd expect. [7:03] truck. At the big box store, small surfaced boards of oak, maple, and walnut typically work out to somewhere around $6 to $12 a board foot once you do the math on the tag. The same species bought rough sawn from a mill or hardwood dealer typically runs around $2 to $5 a board foot. Prices shift with your region and the species, so call a couple of yards and get their current list. But look at that gap. Take a small table that needs 40 board feet. At $8 a

[7:34] board foot, that's $320 of wood. At $3.50, it's 140. More than half the bill gone. It adds up fast. Now the honest part. To use rough stock, you need a way to flatten and thickness it. A benchtop planer runs around 3 to 400 dollars, and paired with the table saw you likely already own, it handles most of the job. If you're saving four or five dollars on every board foot,

[8:04] that planer pays for itself within a few hundred board feet of lumber. After that, the savings are yours on every project. And if you already own a planer, you've been sitting on this discount the whole time. Do the math once on paper, and the choice pretty much makes itself. Fifth on the list. Go to the yard prepared with three things in your pocket and one rule in your head. First, bring a tape measure so you can check widths and lengths and figure board feet right there at the stack. Second, bring

[8:36] a moisture meter. A basic pin-type meter doesn't cost much, and it tells you how much water is still in the wood. For furniture that lives indoors, you want boards reading somewhere between 6 and 9%. Push the pins in a few inches from the end and toward the middle of the board, not right at the tip because the ends dry faster and will fool you. If a board reads well above that range, it isn't ready for your shop yet, no matter how good it looks. Ask the dealer whether the stock is kiln-dried or

[9:07] air-dried because air-dried lumber often needs more time before it settles down indoors. Third, bring your cut list with the board feet already figured. Now the rule. Buy a little long and a little extra. Rough boards often have checks at the ends, which are small cracks, and you'll trim those off. If your part is 30 inches, don't buy a 31-inch board. Buy one that leaves you a few inches at each end. And for any part that's going to be milled anyway, buy it rough. There's no

[9:39] sense paying for surfacing you're about to plane right back off. While you're at the stack, sight down each board like a rifle barrel. Then look at the end grain. Growth rings running straight up and down through the thickness mean a board that tends to stay flat. Rings curving across the end like a smile mean a board more likely to cup, which is when the face dishes across its width. A few minutes at the rack saves you from a board that fights you all the way through the build. Worth every minute.

[10:10] Step six is the patient one. Let the lumber settle in your shop before you cut it. Wood that came from a yard, a barn, or an open shed has been living in different air than your garage or basement. Bring it home and mill it the same afternoon and it can move on you after you've already flattened it. A board you planed dead flat on Saturday can have a bow in it by Wednesday. So, sticker it. Stickering means stacking the boards flat with thin, dry sticks between each layer. The sticks should be

[10:42] about 3/4 of an inch square, spaced every foot and a half or so, and lined up one above the other. That lets air move evenly around every face. Keep the stack off bare concrete, out of direct sun, and away from the furnace. Give it a week or two, check it with your moisture meter, and when the readings hold steady in your target range, it's ready. Here's one habit I'd pass along. Rough cut your parts a couple of inches oversize before they rest. Shorter

[11:12] pieces tend to settle sooner, and any movement shows up before final milling instead of after. Your wood tells you when it's ready if you let it. Patience is cheaper than a second board. Here comes number seven, the one I told you to wait for. Mill rough lumber in the right order without fail. Face, face, edge, edge, length. Get that order wrong and you'll push a twisted board through the planer and it'll come out the other end smooth, thinner, and still twisted.

[11:43] A planer makes the second face parallel to the first. It doesn't make anything flat on its own. So, start with one flat face. If you have a jointer, that's its job. If you don't, you have two good options. One is a planer sled, which is a flat carrier like a piece of melamine or plywood that your rough board rides on. Set the board on the sled and slip thin shims under any spot where it rocks so it can't flex under the rollers. Hot

[12:13] glue the shims in place, then run the whole sled through the planer taking light passes until the top face is flat. The other option is a hand plane. A number five jack plane works across the high spots until a straight edge sits flat on the face all the way around. Either way, mark the flat face with a pencil squiggles so you always know which side is your reference. Next, flip the board and plane the second face with the flat side down taking light passes of a 64th to a 32nd of an inch at a time

[12:46] until you reach your thickness. Take a little off each face as you go rather than hogging it all from one side so the board stays balanced. Expect the planer to leave a slight dip at each end called snipe. That's one more reason you bought a little long. Now the first edge. Without a jointer, make a straight line rip. Screw or clamp your rough board to a straight carrier like a strip of plywood with a factory edge with the rough edge hanging just past the carrier on the blade side. Run

[13:17] the carrier's factory edge along the table saw fence and the blade cuts a clean straight edge on your board. Then flip the board so that new straight edge rides the fence and rip it to final width. Crosscut to length last. Face, face, edge, edge, length. Say it out loud a few times. Do it in that order and a crooked pile of rough boards turns into flat, square stock that looks like it came off a store rack, only better, for about half the money.

[13:48] The eighth and last numbered step, mill it safely. Because rough lumber behaves differently from store boards. Start at the table saw. A rough board can pinch the blade as tension inside the wood releases during the cut, and that's where kickback comes from. Kickback is when the saw grabs the board and throws it back toward you, hard and fast. So, keep the riving knife installed. That's the curved metal fin right behind the blade that keeps the cut from closing on the back of the

[14:19] blade. Use push sticks once your hand would come within about 6 in of the blade. Stand a little to the side of the board's path, not directly behind it. And never use the rip fence as a stop when you crosscut. A short piece trapped between the fence and a spinning blade has nowhere to go but back at you. Use a miter gauge or a crosscut sled instead. If you need a length stop, clamp a block to the fence ahead of the blade so the piece is free before it reaches the cut.

[14:50] That rule doesn't bend. Over at the planer, hook up dust collection. Rough stock throws off a startling pile of chips. A planer without collection clogs, presses chips back into the board, and fills your shop with fine dust you don't want to be breathing. Wear eye and hearing protection, too, because planers are loud. Check your planer's manual for the shortest board it can safely take, and never feed anything shorter. Before any rough board touches a blade, run your

[15:22] hand and your eyes over it for nails, staples, or embedded grit, especially with urban lumber, since town trees sometimes hold old fence nails. A metal detector wand is a cheap way to save a blade. Your fingers are worth more than any board. And now the bonus I promised. Before you pay, ask to see the cull pile and the shorts bin. Culls are boards the yard graded down for a knot, a split, some sapwood, or a little wane, which is bark edge left

[15:52] along the side. Shorts are pieces too short for the main racks. Both usually sell for less than the regular stock, sometimes a good deal less. Here's what a lot of folks miss. Most projects don't need long, perfect boards. Drawer fronts, boxes, frame parts, shelf brackets, legs, and small tables all come out of short stock, and a knot you'd cut around anyway costs you nothing. I've built some of my favorite small pieces entirely out of a shorts bin, and

[16:23] nobody who's seen them has ever guessed. Bring your cut list, match the short parts to short boards, and save the long, clear stock for the places that need it. That one habit stretches every dollar you spend at the yard. If today's trip to the sawmill has you wondering what to build with that stack, my digital book, The Ultimate DIY Woodworking Guide, walks through complete furniture projects from the first cut to the final coat. It follows every technique with a breakdown of the

[16:53] common mistakes, so you can catch them before they cost you a board. You can grab it by scanning the code on your screen or tapping the first link in the description. And if you want more shop-tested fixes and beginner-safe techniques like these, subscribe to Dale Oakfield's Workshop, and tell me in the comments what you'd build first from a load of rough-sawn oak. Now go find your nearest sawmill and bring home something worth milling.

About six 8-ft 2 by 4s, and take a minute to pick straight ones by sighting down the edge like you're aiming a pool cue. Four carriage bolts, 3/8 in thick

[6:08] and about 4 in long with a washer and a nylon lock nut for each. The lock nuts are the ones with the little plastic ring inside. A box of 3-in construction screws, a box of 1 and 1/4-in screws, wood glue, wood filler, primer, whatever paint colors you're after, and a can of water-based polyurethane. You probably already own most of the tools. Circular saw, drill, jigsaw, a tape measure, a square, a couple of clamps, and sandpaper. Now, the 1/2-in or 3/4

[6:40] question. A 2 by 4 standing on its edge is 3 and 1/2 in tall. Put a 1/2-in top on it, and the front of your board sits at exactly 4 in, right at the top of regulation. Put a 3/4-in top on it, and you're at 4 and 1/4. Nobody at a tailgate has ever pulled a tape measure on that, and 3/4 feels a little more solid under a hard throw. But, if a buyer tells you they want regulation and they mean it, go with 1/2 in. Either way, you're adding a center

[7:10] brace, and we'll get to that. Step one is breaking down the sheet, and this is where I slow down, and so should you. A full sheet of plywood is heavy, it's awkward, and if you cut it across two sawhorses, the piece you're cutting off sags, pinches the blade, and the saw can kick back at you. So, don't do that. Lay the whole sheet flat on the garage floor on top of a sheet of rigid foam insulation or on a grid of 2 by 4s spaced about a foot apart. That way both sides of the cut stay

[7:40] supported the whole way through, and nothing drops. Set your blade depth just a hair deeper than the plywood, about a quarter inch past the bottom. That's the safer setting because less blade is hanging out underneath and the cut stays cleaner. Safety glasses on, ear protection on. Clamp a straight scrap to the sheet as a fence. Run the saw along it and let the saw do the work instead of shoving it. Rip the sheet down the middle lengthwise so you've got two strips about 24 inches wide. Then cross cut each strip at 48

[8:11] inches. That gives you four tops from one sheet, which is enough for two sets. Just know the pair from the second strip comes out a hair under 24 because the blade eats about an eighth of an inch. I still price a full sheet into every set because I want the math to be boring and honest and the leftovers are a head start on the next order. Done with the big cuts. Sweep up and let's build frames. Step two, the frames. For each board, you need two long sides at 48 inches, two short ends at 21

[8:43] inches, and one center brace, also at 21 inches. Why 21? A 2 by 4 is an inch and a half thick. So, two end pieces tucked between the long sides eat 3 inches of your 24. 24 minus 3 is 21. That's all the math there is. Cut all 10 pieces for both boards at once. Mark them, square your lines, and cut. Stand the long sides on edge, tuck the short ends in between them, run a bead of glue on each joint, and pre-drill

[9:13] before you drive screws near the end of a board. If you skip the pilot hole on a stud end, it'll split right on you and then you're back at the store buying another 2 by 4. Two 3-inch screws per corner through the long side into the end grain of the short piece. Then drop the center brace in at the 24-inch mark, glue it, and screw it the same way. That brace keeps the top from drumming and flexing when a bag lands, which matters a lot with half-inch plywood, and still helps with 3/4. Before the glue grabs, check the frame for square.

[9:45] Measure corner to corner both ways. If the two measurements match, you're square. If they don't, push the long corner in gently until they do. Takes about 30 seconds. Skip it, and your top won't sit flush. And that's the difference between something that looks homemade and something that looks handmade. Buyers can tell, even if they couldn't tell you why. Step three, the top. Run a bead of glue along the top edge of the whole frame, brace included, and lay the plywood on with the good face up. Line up the edges

[10:16] flush all the way around. Clamp a corner if it wants to wander. Then drive 1 and 1/4-in screws down through the top into the frame every 8 in or so, and countersink them just below the surface so you can fill over them. Fill every screw hole with wood filler, let it dry, and sand it flat. Painted boards show every little dimple, so don't rush this part. Which brings us to step four, the hole, which is the step everybody gets nervous about. Don't be. Measure 9 in down from the top edge and

[10:47] 12 in in from either side and mark the spot. That's your center. For the circle, you don't need a fancy compass. Grab a scrap stick, drive a small nail through it, and drill a pencil-sized hole exactly 3 in from the nail. Tap the nail into your center mark, stick a pencil in the other hole, and swing it around. Now you've got a 6-in circle. Now drill a starter hole inside the line, 1/2 in or so, just big enough to fit your jigsaw blade through. Drop the blade in, keep the saw's base flat

[11:18] on the plywood, and follow just inside your pencil line nice and slow. A fine-tooth blade gives you less splintering on the good face. If your hands aren't steady yet, that's fine. Stop, take a breath, and keep going, because a wobbly cut sands out, and a rushed cut doesn't. Once the plug drops out, wrap sandpaper around a finger and soften the edge of the hole all the way around, so bags don't snag and nobody's knuckles do, either. Step five, the legs. And this is the step with the real

[11:48] safety point in it, so pay attention here. Each board gets two legs at the back end, made from 2 by 4, about a foot long to start. Here's the easy way to get the angle right without doing trig. Flip the board right side up and prop the back edge on blocks or a stack of scrap until the top of the back edge sits 12 inches off the floor. Now, hold the leg straight down inside the frame at one back corner, snug against the frame side, and lay a pencil flat on the floor against the leg. Trace a line.

[12:18] That line is exactly the angle your foot needs to sit flat on the ground. Cut it, test it, then use that leg as the pattern for the other three. For the pivot, clamp the leg in position and drill a 3/8-in hole straight through the frame side and the leg together, about 2 inches down from the top of the frame and about 2 inches in from the back end. Push the carriage bolt through from the outside, so the smooth rounded head faces out. Then, put on a washer and a nylon locknut on the inside. Tap

[12:49] the bolt head with a hammer, so the square neck bites into the wood and the bolt can't spin. Tighten the nut until the leg swings with some resistance, but doesn't flop. Here's why I'm fussy about that nut. A regular nut backs itself off a little every time the leg folds. And one day, at a family reunion, it lets go. The leg folds under the board, and somebody's uncle eats plywood. The nylon ring in a locknut grips the thread, so it stays put through fold after fold. It's a small piece of hardware doing a

[13:19] big job, so don't swap it out to save a few cents. Round over the top end of each leg with the jigsaw and a little sanding, so it can swing down without binding against the underside of the top. Then, open the leg to where the foot sits flat, and screw a small scrap block to the inside of the frame right where the leg stops, so it can't swing any farther than it should. Stand the board up and lean on the back like somebody reaching for a bag that rolled under it. It shouldn't budge. If it does, fix it now, not after it's in a

[13:49] stranger's backyard. Step six, the finish. And this is where the set starts looking like something people will pay for. Sand the whole top smooth, starting at 120 grit and finishing at 180, and ease every edge so nothing sharp to grab. Wipe off the dust. Prime it. Primer is what makes paint look even instead of blotchy on plywood, and it takes a few extra minutes. Then, two coats of your color. Designs are just painter's tape and patience. A stripe down the middle, a border around the

[14:20] edge, a big diamond around the hole. Press the tape edges down hard with a fingernail. Paint, and pull the tape while the last coat is still a little soft, so you get a clean line. For names and dates, you can cut a stencil, buy one, or freehand it if you've got the hand for it. I don't, so I cheat with stencils, and so far, nobody's complained. Then, seal it. Several thin coats of water-based polyurethane, a light pass with fine sandpaper between coats, and let it cure as long as the

[14:50] can tells you before any bags hit it. The poly protects the paint, and it gives the surface the slide players expect. Rush the cure, and bags will stick and leave marks in soft finish, and your first review will be about bag marks. Let it sit. Go mow the lawn. It's done when the can says it's done. Step seven, the price. And here's the question I promised you. When somebody messages you about a set, before you say a word about money, you ask one thing. Who's name goes on it? Or, what's the

[15:22] date? That one question changes the conversation. Now they're not shopping for cornhole boards, they're picturing their last name across the top at the family reunion, or their wedding date sitting in the grass at the reception. Personalized is the word that makes people pay more, and asking that question first is how you get there. A plain set is something they'll compare against the next listing. A set with their name on it doesn't have anything to compare to. That's how a $200 set becomes a $300 set, and all it cost you was a stencil and a little extra paint.

[15:53] Step eight, the part that fills the jar. Take good photos. I staged mine out back on the grass with a couple of bags half in the hole and my half-dead succulents off to the side trying their best. And honestly, the succulents are doing more work than you'd think. Post on your local marketplace listings, in wedding and event groups around town, and anywhere booster club parents hang out, because school colors with a family name sell every single fall. Build one display set in a bold color and bring it to a craft fair, and take orders there

[16:24] instead of hauling 10 sets around. And batch everything. Cut frames for four sets in one session. Drill all the leg pivots in one session. Paint everything in one long afternoon while the shop radio plays whatever it plays. At about 180 minutes of hands-on work per set, 25 sets comes to around 75 hours of actual building, spread out over a few seasons, one or two orders a month. When the shelf is empty and people are still asking, that's your sign it's working.

[16:54] 20-some sets later, the jar is full, and every dollar of that trip came out of plywood and studs. [8:43] how a whole post's worth goes, start to finish. It starts at the lumber rack. And the first thing to check is printed right on the tag. Buy untreated. Pressure-treated 4x4s are made to sit in the ground and out in the weather, and they belong outside, never inside somebody's house. While you're there, sight down a few posts and take the straightest one with no long split running down the face. And if the post feels heavy and damp, it probably came off a wet lumber yard. Let it sit in the shop a week or two before you cut

[9:14] because a wet post can crack as it dries. And a crack that runs into the pinhole ruins the block. At the saw, trim the factory end square first because it's usually rough or cracked. Then, clamp a stop block to the fence 7 and 1/2 inches from the blade, butt the post against it, and cut. Slide it down, cut again, and every block comes out the same height without one more pencil mark. One warning on that setup though. The block you just cut sits trapped between the blade and the stop. So, let the blade quit spinning

[9:46] before you lift it or it can grab that block and throw it. And if your saw won't make it through a 4x4 in one pass, cut, roll the post over and finish from the other side and the stop block keeps both cuts lined up. Then, stand each block on the bench and give it a little push. If it rocks, the cut wasn't square. And a rocking block can tip with a candle on it. So, sand the bottom flat or set that one aside. Next come the corners and here's that choice I promised you. With a router and a chamfer bit, the kind with a little

[10:17] bearing that rides along the edge, a couple of light passes take the sharp off each edge in no time. Set the bit shallow and sneak up on the depth you like. Do the edges around the top and bottom first, then the four long ones because end grain likes to chip at the corners and the last pass is clean that up. But a block this size is a small thing to be holding next to a bit spinning that fast. So, don't hand hold it. Clamp it to the bench or run it on a router table behind a push block and keep your fingers well back. If any of

[10:48] that makes your stomach tighten, grab a sander instead. Tilt the sander or the block so the sandpaper meets each edge at an angle. Work every edge the same amount and you'll get the same look with nothing in the room that can bite you. It takes longer and that's a trade I'd make every single time. Hang on. Let me knock the dust off this one. Then the pin hole and this is the step I'd ask you to slow down for. The pin has to land dead center because a 3-in pillar candle is going to sit on it and if that pin wanders to one side

[11:20] or leans, the candle leans right along with it. A leaning candle drips down the side and puts the flame closer to the wood than you want it. So, make a drilling jig once and use it on every block. Mine's nothing fancy. A scrap cut to the size of the post end, marked corner to corner with an X, drilled straight through where the lines cross with a thin strip tacked on two sides so it hooks square over the end of each block. [11:52] a test hole in a scrap first to check the fit, and wrap a little flag of tape around the bit so every hole stops at the same depth. Then tap the pin in snug so it doesn't wobble. [12:53] doesn't dry sticky. Keep in mind the top and bottom of each block are end grain, and end grain drink stain. So those faces come out darker than the sides. Some buyers like that. [7:06] board with a little breathing room for the saw blade. On a miter saw, measure 11 in from the blade and clamp a scrap block to the fence right there. That's your stop block. Now every top is the same length and you never pick up a tape measure again. Slide the board up to the block. Hold the long end, cut, and let the blade stop completely before you lift it. [7:36] a time. Now, the legs, and this is where I want you paying attention. The legs are short blocks, four to 5 in tall. And I make mine from the offcut pile. The mistake I see over and over is somebody holding a tiny 4-in block with their fingertips and pushing it into a spinning blade. Please don't do that. Your hand ends up inches from the teeth, and if that little block catches, it

[8:07] goes wherever it wants and takes your hand along for the ride. The safe way is to never cut a leg from a short piece. Cut it from the end of a long one. Set a second stop block at your leg length, say 4 and 1/2 in, and clamp it down tight. Slide the longboard up against it. Hold the long end well away from the blade and make your cut. Let the blade come to a full stop, raise it, and only then reach in and slide the leg away. Your hand stays on the long side of the board the whole time. When the board

[8:38] you're cutting from gets down to about a foot long, stop. That last piece goes back in the scrap bin. It's not worth your fingers. And I'm going to slow down here because the circular saw is the tool that bites beginners and it almost never bites the way folks expect. The thing to understand is kickback. That's when the cut closes up behind the blade and pinches it and the saw jumps back toward you hard and fast. It almost always

[8:41] comes from how the board is supported, not from the saw. Picture a board resting across two saw horses and you cut right in the middle between them. The board sags as you cut. The two halves fold in toward each other. The cut squeezes shut and the blade gets pinched. Or picture cutting a piece off the end with the scrap hanging in the air. It drops, twists, and grabs the blade on the way down. The fix is to support both sides of every cut. The easiest beginner way I know is a sheet of rigid foam insulation laid flat on

[9:13] the floor or on a sturdy table. You set the 2x4 on the foam. Set your blade to cut just a/4 in deeper than the board and cut right through into the foam. Both sides of the cut are sitting on something the whole time so nothing sags and nothing drops. Foam's cheap. It lasts a long while and it's the best safety gear most beginners never think to buy. [9:43] the edge of nothing. Then clamp the work down every cut. A board that can scoot is a board that can surprise you. And before you ever plug that saw in, check the lower blade guard. On pallets that have crossed a border, you will find a burned-in

[2:02] stamp with some letters. HT means heat treated, which is just wood baked in a kiln until whatever was living in it was not living in it anymore. That wood is fine. KD means kiln dried, also fine, and you will often see KDHT together. DB means debarked, nothing to worry about. Those are your yes pallets. MB is the one you walk away from. That stands for methyl bromide, a fumigant, nasty enough that most of the world phased it out years ago. Phased out is not the same as gone, though, and older pallets still

[2:32] float around. If you find those two letters, leave it and do not burn it, either, which people never think about. [4:02] deck board and the stringer and cut the nails right off. Board comes free, clean, full length, zero splits. A five pack of blades runs about $15 and gets you through a lot of pallets.
